St Petersburg → Warsaw · one-part code of 1783 · December 1786 – October 1787 · read with the key of the time

Deboli and Gołuchowski, St Petersburg, 1786–87

Go to the full text ↓

The Polish envoy in St Petersburg reports on the Russian regiments in Poland, Potemkin and the Empress’s journey south, in a code whose key of 1783 survives and was applied here blind.

Daniel Bourdeau · posted 6 November 2026

Method: read with known key · Extent: partial (82% of the cipher) · On the map

With Brice Dudout (solution) · Also credited · Contemporary key or decipherment (key or decipherment used) · Daniel Bourdeau (pointer to a source)

known key
On this pageThe despatchesThe key and its testThe readingWhat remains uncertainFull textsSources

At a glanceKnown key applied · partial (82% of the cipher)

The despatches
Eleven despatches of Antoni Augustyn Deboli, Polish envoy in St Petersburg, to King Stanisław August (29 December 1786 – 16 October 1787), and three letters of Gołuchowski to one of the King’s ministers, all in the older code.
Where
AGAD Warsaw, Zbiór Popielów (zesp. 12), sygn. 3, “Deboli. 1787 (cyfry)”, 83 pieces; the key in sygn. 444. Not on DECODE.
The cipher
A one-part Polish code in shuffled letter blocks: groups 1–1969 for syllables, words and names, signal group 2345, nulls from 1970. The same volume also uses tables 9933, 3865, 12282 and the code of 1789–91, which are not deciphered here.
How it was deciphered
With the key of the time, “Cyfra polska” of 8 March 1783, transcribed in full (1,965 entries) and applied without changing a value from the sense. Read blind: the cabinet’s clear texts of 1786–87 were not opened.
What it says
Deboli presses Vice-Chancellor Ostermann for the withdrawal of the Russian regiment stationed in Poland, as the Sejm instructed; everything depends on Potemkin; the Empress’s journey and Joseph II’s meeting with her.
Still open
1,281 unresolved words, partly misread digits and partly values that do not fit; the key scores 0.837 on a held-out 1788 pair, under the 0.90 set in advance; the pieces in the other tables.
Credit
Brice Dudout (Europe chiffrée) transcribed the key and the despatches and read them, working with Claude Opus 5.5 agents. The key itself is the cabinet’s of 1783.

01 The despatches

Volume 3 of the Zbiór Popielów holds Deboli’s cipher despatches of 1787, 83 pieces, and four inserted letters of Gołuchowski. Sorted by their opening group, 22 pieces are in the older code (signal 2345), 19 in table 9933, 14 in table 3865, 3 in table 12282, 3 in the code of 1789–91 and 3 mostly in clear. The fourteen deciphered here are the older-code pieces that were transcribed. The code of 1789–91 has its own pages (Deboli 1789, Deboli 1788).

The cabinet’s clear texts of 1786–87 are in AGAD, Archiwum Królestwa Polskiego 269. By its header index, a clear of the same date exists for 8 of the 14 pieces; for those the content is probably already known, and the reading here is a blind reading of them, not a first one. For six pieces (p13, p21, p28, p33, g1, g3) no clear text was found.

02 The key and its test

Sygn. 444, “Cyfra polska”, 8 March 1783, is a one-part code: blocks of groups for each initial letter in shuffled order (M from 1, N from 92, O from 211, P from 326 … D from 1323), endings and alternatives written with the values. It was transcribed from its nine scans (98% legible) and re-read on the image only for 21 values; no value was changed from the sense of a despatch. It is not the code of 1789–91 (8 is mi here, do there).

The key was tested on three despatches of 1788 in the older code against their cabinet decipherments, the ciphers hashed before the clears: 0.874, 0.874 and 0.837 on the held-out one, against shuffled-key controls of 0.08–0.12. The threshold fixed in advance was 0.90, so the test failed at that level while confirming 444 as the key of 1783–88; most misses are alignment artefacts and misread digits.

03 The reading

Counted by script on each reading file, the fourteen pieces come to 7,040 words: 5,499 certain, 256 probable, 4 restored and 1,281 unresolved, 81.8% read by sense. The share per piece runs from 0.70 to 0.91, so none reaches the 95% bar. The unresolved syllables are left in place. A few digits were settled by blind shape questions answered by Brice Dudout. The readings, in Polish or French, are under Full texts; the English file gives summaries of the deciphered parts, not full translations.

Deboli tells the King that Ostermann’s evasive answer about the Russian regiments in Poland kept him from asking formally; he presses for their withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him, and judges that everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a commission he fears. Other pieces follow the Empress’s journey and Joseph II’s plan to meet her at Kherson rather than Kiev.

04 What remains uncertain

  • 1,281 unresolved words, isolated syllables that do not construe: partly misread digits of the despatches, partly values of 444 that do not fit, and four places where the key itself is torn or rubbed out (1215, 1216, 1398, 1405).
  • The key fails its own test at 0.90 (0.837 on the held-out pair); a new test on three despatches of 1786 with clears in AKP 269 is pre-registered and not yet run.
  • The digits were transcribed once, from the scans; blind questions were used only where one digit changes the reading.
  • The pieces in tables 9933, 3865 and 12282 and in the code of 1789–91 are not deciphered; no key of the time for them was found.
  • For eight pieces the cabinet’s clear texts exist and were deliberately not opened, so the reading has not been compared with them.

Full texts

The transcription and the decryption of every document on this page, so that the reading can be used and checked. Short files are shown here; all are in the repository.

piece p01, Deboli, 29 Dec 1786 (received 9 Jan 1787) read in part

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (83% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

Reading

p01.md

# deboli1787 p01 - reading by sense (Deboli, 29 Dec 1786 (received 9 Jan 1787))

Marks: plain = certain; {x} = probable; [x] = restored; ‹x› = unresolved (decoded syllables that make no sense in place).
Spelling of the time kept. Count (counted by script on this file, restored words excluded): 430 words, 316 certain,
42 probable, 0 restored, 72 unresolved -> 0.833: read in part.

Wychodzi prawie na to, {iuż} com był powiedział z odpowiedzi Podkanclerzego względem Regimentu, o ktorey {doniosłem} WKMci w liście moim de 22. elapsi.

{Obojętność} owey odpowiedzi wstrzymała mię była {podówczas} od uczynienia formalnego zapytania, ponieważ bałem się, abym przez to nie przyspieszył wyraźnego w tey mierze {odmowienia}.

A takowego ‹wzyręcz› za dyskretnie ‹dzenie› sposobem ‹zni›, ‹1216› przy ‹1219› rzeczy dlatego ‹pozadan› ‹okoliczności› ‹aią›, że przez to w {oczekiwaniu} się przecie {zostawało}, iż azali ‹cos› ‹zawstrzyma› {obiecywać} ‹sosie› będzie {można} z kroku uczynionego do Xięcia Potemkina, przez posłanie do niego ‹offiara› ‹er› a z {przełożeniem} ‹niema› {gwałtow} przez ‹owa› Regimenta {popełnionych}.

Tym czasem ia tu zakładałem ‹sosie› ‹nia› nie {zaniedbywać}, coby mię mogło {doprowadzić} do ‹nieznaionego› {wyrozumienia}, iak tu {zamysłaią} {wyprowadzić} od ‹narażi› ‹wspaniałom› ‹niony› {Regimentu}, lubo też go {zostawić}.

W tym zamiarze w przeszłą {Środę} różnemi sposobami nacierałem na Podkanclerzego, abym co z niego w tey materyi {wyczerpnął}; ‹aliście› był {łaskawy} nie iak nie ‹Audiencya›; ani mi powiedział, że Regimenta {woydzie}, ani negative; ale w tym {znaczeniu} iuż mi brać potrzeba ‹Czechy› samo milczenie.

Ja ‹mgo› pierwszy {przestrzegał}, iuż po odebraniu depeszy WKMci de 16 currentis. W tey okazyi widziałem iaśnie, że nie są dotąd {dysponowani} do wyciągnienia ‹oddodać› Regimentów, y że {nawet} dokąd {naglić} będę wyraźnie mi to {powiedzą}.

Już tedy ‹przedoświadczonych› prawie iestem u siebie o tym, y {przyidzie} na to, że na naypierwszey konferencyi z takową odpowiedzią {odezwą} się, a ia będę przymuszony przy niektorych ‹grodzkich› {oświadczyć} z treści tego de die WKMci depeszy Departamentowey pod datą 9 currentis wyrazow, y przydać wszystko co będę mogł {sądzić} {przyzwoitym} do powiedzenia tu ‹Sardynia› y; między innemi, że ‹niechay› ‹sną› będzie rzecz dla ‹narażi›, że Cesarz w drodze swoiey do Kijowa oczyma {swemi} widzieć będzie Regimenta Rossyiskie w naszym Kraiu.

Przybliża się czas, w ktorym mi ‹wyia› ‹dać› Imperatorowey ‹naznaczony› ‹utrzym› ‹uie› w ‹niech› y ‹bne› y pewność tę ‹podroz›, o ktorey ‹co› Buchholtz {rozgłasza} iest ‹szczeguln› ‹rym› fałsz.

Do ktorego ‹Cru› ‹Tta› był u ‹mnie› y sam {mi} wy{explikował} swoie interessa, {sprawiedliwie} y {prawdziwie}, ale tak {długo} ciąg{ną} ‹cya› się, że choć będę ‹cznie› iego mowić na mało się to przyda.

{Memoriał} Hrabi ‹Tartarya› ‹nuWskiego› ‹zawierny› a tym ‹sosie› tak interessa, o ktorych ‹mwzy› ‹miał› ‹91› ‹kipra› ‹wiele› czynić nie można, bo by tak ‹aią› rzecz wiele familij Polskich ‹miał› y ‹powpodoba› mi ‹nania› się o {różnych} miastach y ‹wsiE› w ‹Ukrainie› ‹Ruskiey› ‹Syberya› ‹tiowane› między ‹sukce› ‹sosorowiele› ‹wisniowiel› ‹ckich› ‹obatiry› m ktory należał ‹da› w niey do tey familii.

Do Departamentu pro formaln dziś tylko piszę, bo Bog widzi nie wiele mam co iuż pisać o Regimentach.

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

piece p04, Deboli, 9 Jan 1787 read in part

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (70% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# deboli1787 p04 - reading by sense (Deboli, 9 Jan 1787)

Marks: plain = certain; {x} = probable; [x] = restored; ‹x› = unresolved (decoded syllables that make no sense in place).
Spelling of the time kept. Count (counted by script on this file, restored words excluded): 337 words, 216 certain,
18 probable, 4 restored, 99 unresolved -> 0.703: read in part.

Da się widzieć , byle za odpowiedzią {od} ‹biorę› na iutrzeyszey konferencyi na list W.KrMci de 16 ‹dziu› ‹ap› ‹si› .

{Podług} tego iak do mnie przemowić {będę} ‹stolica› ‹sę› ‹wa› ‹ł› ‹mo› ie reprezentacya zawsze do tego dążące , aby się pozbyć {nareszcie} tego Regimentu .

Wyiazd łaskawey Imperatorowey iednostaynie przy tym się dotąd zostaie , co iuż dawniey ‹ni› ‹M› ogłoszono .

‹wygr› ‹ta› ‹n› ‹dem› po przyieździe ostatniego Kuryera z Wiednia , że Cesarz {obrał} {się} przyiechać ‹rzeczy› do Chersonu , niż do Kijowa .

‹a› ‹assada› ‹Anglia› ‹ka› ‹pu› szczegulnie ‹a› ‹pomierzeście› od głosu , iakoby tam Cesarz nie będzie się mógł długo bawić , z przyczyny maiącego nastąpić przybycia do Wiednia Krolestwa {Neapolitańskich} ‹mę› ‹Poniatowski› .

Xiąże Potemkin ‹nieni› iuż {z} przyiedzie ‹14›

do {Krzyczewa} dla przyięcia Imperatorowey , a będzie {ią} {czekał} w Kijowie , bo inaczey nie mógłby skończyć {objazdki} , ktore teraz ‹zaprza› ‹aią› ‹tnio› ‹nagrod› .

‹nie› ‹wyraz› Xiężna Würtembergskiey {an} {xra} nie {przestaie} martwić Xięstwo .

‹Mandat› ‹1› 6 ‹el› ‹ap› ‹si› nie ‹wo› ‹choć› ‹dz› ‹a› dotąd z swoich ‹1108› ‹ar› ‹Sardynia› ‹me› ‹n› ‹yły› .

Iuż była prawie głośno rezolucya , że miała ‹no› odebrać ‹sta› ‹c› ‹wyd› ‹om› ‹niszczy› ‹ną› Xiężna do ‹rawela› , aby tam pod niebytność Imperatorowey ‹przen› ‹Lublin› ‹do› ‹poki› by nie odebrała odpowiedzi od Xięcia [Brunswickiego] , ktore y nadto dla iego ‹co› ‹rki› {pomyślne} ‹y› {sposobem} ‹dz› ‹wac› się prawie nie można ,

{są} ‹dz› ‹aiąc› potym co iey iuż ‹w› ‹pocz› ‹aią› wysoki ‹Katolik› ‹wym› ‹z› ‹mę› że ‹m› ‹porozn› ‹ie› ‹ambit› pisał : zalecaiąc iey , aby się starała ‹ziemi› ‹d› znać ‹sę› ‹sie› affekt iego , bo inaczey on Oyciec iey stronę [bynaymniey] brać nie ‹Podole› ; ani też ią do siebie wokować .

Niechby się ‹1216› na tym zostawała , aby ią do ‹rowelu› odebrać ‹sta› ‹c› , ‹au› od [dnia] wczorayszego mowią , zaczęto , że ią Imperatorowa z sobą bierze : coby naturalnie ie bardzo ‹Katolik› dolegało .

‹Wilno› Generał ‹ma› ‹mo› ‹no› w zapadł był w Sobotę dość mocno , ale przecie lepiey się ma : inaczey mógłby to ‹1216› z Wielce ‹Y› ‹wi› ‹ia› ‹z› .

List W.KrMci de ‹00› 7 Decemb. [dnia] wczorayszego odebrałem .

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

Przez 3 Poczty consecutive nie miałem żadney od Jasnie Wielmożnego Dobrodzieia {odebrać} ‹z› ‹wy› . Ja czyniąc {dzisieyszą} ‹aią› nie mam ‹gł› ‹ya› do doniesienia , iak tylko , że względem responsu na list Nayiaśnieyszego Pana do Imperatorowey , trzeba mniemać , iż od niey samey wyidzie , ponieważ , iak em się wywiedział , żadnych w tey mierze nie dała monarchyni rozkazów kancellaryi grafa Ostermanna , przy zwróceniu do niey depesz Pana {Składowskiego} , do których y ‹wspaniałom› ‹nio› ‹ny› list był przyłączony .

Poseł Angielski iuż z tąd wyiechał . Małe , podług moich wiadomości , iest podobieństwo do iego {powrotu} , y prawie zadnego , ponieważ w mniemaniu odda‹la› ł się z tąd na czas , a przeto podług ‹et› y ‹kiedy› tu nie wypadło , aby odebrać iako ‹wy› {prezent} ; chcą ‹C› ‹przeciwko› ‹E› ‹U› ‹czyni› ‹no› iakową dyskursy ‹ty› ‹N› ‹K› ‹cya› , rzecz tak była {nakierowana} , że gdzie ‹ma› ‹mo› ‹no› w ‹po› ‹ka› ‹za› ‹wszy› ‹mu› ‹w› ‹przod› portret Imperatorowey , niby to dla wyciągnienia {zdatn} ‹nia› , Fitzherberta {osobistość} był podobny , zostawił ten że sam portret w ręku iego w przytomności Imperatorowey .

We Wtorek reprezentowana była w Hermitażu tragedya pod tytułem Koryolan , kompozycyi Pana Segura , z Ministrów Cudzoziemskich nie byli wezwani , iak tylko Kobentzel y sam autor . Co dzień prawie na małym teatrze bywaią grane komedye lubo proverbes ; na których Imperatorowa bywa w małey Kompanii . We Czwartki zaś bywaią y drudzy Ministrowie Cudzoziemscy , dawnym zwyczaiem ‹1398› .

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

Depesza moia dzisieysza do Departamentu iest tylko pro forma ; ‹za› ‹yi› a iednak w sobie ‹trze› ‹E› ‹ści› tego , co mi w samey rzeczy powiedziano ; z tym zaś , co miałem pisać do Pana ‹skła› , ‹obowiąz›‹ek› ‹bieda› ‹1215› ‹m› do sekretu ‹przydat›‹kiedy› ‹m› , aby ‹m› to tylko W. Kr. Mci doniosł . Y tak sobie postąpiłem w ‹lepiey› ‹cie› de 12 currentis przez Kuryera ‹Austryac›‹kiedy› go posłanego . Potym ‹lepiey› ‹cie› pisałem na zaiutrz drugi przez Kuryera , maiącego bydż wyprawionym do Pana ‹skła› , który to Kuryer nie był ieszcze expedyowany dziś rano , ale iuż depesze dla niego {pieczętowano} .

Wczorayszy w Hermitażu Jmć Pan ‹ma› ‹R› ‹ką› w przechodzący koło mnie rzekł mi te słowa : ‹wą› ‹dobry› ‹ze› ‹s'› ‹raz› z ‹robi› , ze ‹s'› ‹u› ‹czyni› pod Kanclerzemu oświadczenie , które od Imperatorowey ‹·› mile przyięte ‹1216› ; iako z y odpowiedź będzie do tego stosowna . ‹Rozumierzć› się miałem o przez to oświadczenie te , które co do słowa prawie wyraziłem W. Kr. Mci . Co do odpowiedzi , ia ‹m› iey ieszcze nie odebrał , bo ‹m› się w ‹łaskaw›‹y› nie ‹nienabiiał› pod Kanclerzemu w Hermitażu , aby co ze mnie ‹aią› mówiąc a expedycya dla Kuryera , iuż ‹em› był posłał do iego Kancellaryi , ‹ni› ‹me› ‹m› do iż ‹R› mi ta ‹ye› poiechał . W tym w ‹łaskaw› nie mieyscu ‹pospol› ‹ty› ‹gł› ‹em› z rozmowy z Kobentzelem , że on o wyprawie Kuryera do Warszawy nie wiedział : z czym ia się przed nim nie wymówiłem ; bo ‹·› może albo się z tym przed nim taili , albo też on {chciał} ze mnie coś wybadać .

Onegday przyiechał Kuryer Cesarski z ‹wymow› , y maią tu bydż kontenci z przywiezionych przez niego wiadomości : o czym zasłyszałem przez powszechny odgłos , na który się ‹spuszcz›‹ac› nie można . Szczegulności zaś żadnych dotąd nie wiele ‹m› ; to zaś zdaie się mnie samemu rzeczy nie chybną , że w ‹począt›‹aią› ‹T› ‹kach› Cesarz w myśli tuteysze w ‹aią› w padać będzie . Tu ‹szczeguln› ‹rze› woyny teraz ‹niechc›‹aią› . ‹wspaniałom› ‹niony› Minister pytał mię się poufale , iak ia ‹rozumierzm› , czyli Poseł Pruski ‹ra› ‹Bender› w duchu woyny ?
