# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

Dziś dopiero przed połudn‹iema› powróciwszy z Gaczyny , dokończyłem czytanie listu W. Kr. Mci de 12 currentis odebranego w Niedzielę , a który w ten sam ‹1405› nie mógł bydz ze wszystkim ‹·› decyfrowanym przed moim wyiazdem . Pozostały czas aż do odeyścia Poczty iest nadto {szczupły} , aby m mógł w obszerne zapę‹dzać› się pisanie . Odkładam ie więc do przyszłey Poczty , z zamiarem wyłuszczenia w szczegulności teraźnieyszey interessów Dworów europeyskich pozycyi , iako ‹wą› w nay{pozorniey}szym kształcie ‹o› {oznaczaią} ‹wiele› ‹o› ‹ra› ‹kiedy› rozumowania , które tu słyszę .

Tym czasem powiem , że lubo graf Kobentzel nie ‹wymierznia› ieszcze przed zapytuiącemi się go o to Ministrami Cudzoziemskiemi , co z posiłków da Cesarz Rossyi , iuż iednak mówić zaczyna , że ‹·› związki iego z Rossyą są takowe , że wypowiedzenie woyny tey ‹po› ‹693› ‹te› ‹N› ‹cya› przez Turków , wciąga ią y monarchę iego przeciwko nim w woynę . Na tuteyszey ‹·› Radzie ‹roz› ‹mocno› ‹ął› ‹sa› ‹no› kwestyą , czyli wypada Rossyi przestać na małey iakiey korzyści , gdyby ią Turcy offiarowali dla zagładzenia nierozmyślności swoiey w wypowiedzeniu woyny ? ‹ski› ‹ła› ‹da› ‹iący› Radzie prawie wszyscy odezwali się z zdaniem przeciwnym , {popieraiąc} ‹onego› ‹wy› ‹stary› ‹wiatr› ‹niema› pomyślne y w tych czasiech ‹Syberya› ‹ix› ‹ach› Rossyi .

À Capite list , który mi Xiąże de Ligne oddał , do ‹yc› posłałem onegday przez Kuryera Austryackiego . W wczorayszy w Gaczynie na zapytanie Xięcia de Ligne , czyli nie będziemy mieli tego Roku seymu extraordynaryinego , odpowiedziałem , ‹za› ‹wart› rzecz ‹obroni› ‹ci› ‹wszy› , że in quantum seym będzie , będę mu proponował bydz posłanem z Woiewodztwa Wileńskiego : z czego się on śmiał , y na tym się rozmowa skończyła .

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

Na iutrzeyszey konferencyi , dopełniaiąc rozkazu W. Kr. Mci w depeszy de 24 elapsi zawarty proszenia tu , albowiem dla zastonienia Polski dywizya FeldMarszałka Romanzoffa przyspieszyła weyście swoie w granice tureckie , ‹sposob› ‹y› ‹ta› ‹m› się oraz wyraźnie ‹pod› ‹komplet› ‹ego› , na kiedy się {miał} {spodziewać} respons in ordine do zawarcia przymierza z Rossyą {podany} ? że ten respons w robocie , to ‹więk› ‹m› ; a z potocznych dyskursów daie mi się widzieć wachanie się tuteysze na rozpoczęcie ‹m› czegoś u Nas , y że się obawiaią , aby trudności nie doznali z strony Pruskiey : na co ia im tak {odpowiadam} , że zdaie się na to przystawać , to iest : że tu srzodka nie masz pomiędzy weyści ‹el› z nami w umowy , a nieczynnością , która od Nas wzięta będzie za opuszczenie Polski , a przeto przez samo przyspieszy się y ułatwi ‹ści› {intryg} Pruskich .

À Capite . Z wszystkich ich okoliczności miarkuiąc , {naypozorniey} pewność będą w ‹nastąpi› ‹ę› ‹zboże› ‹iących› punktach konkluzye . Nayprzód : że tu długiey ‹żądanie› nie życzą ; a zatym byleby się udało wziąść Oczaków , a ‹tryumf› chcieli , ustępuiąc tey Fortecy , zawrzeć pokoy , tu na niego przystaną ; że ‹ya› Oczaków będą attakować tey iesieni czyli raczey w zimie ; bo tu , iak mi Jmć Pan ‹se› ‹dle› ‹r› powiada , ma bydz {naysposobnieysza} pora do attakowania . Inaczey rzecz trudna , a w bytności eskadry tureckiey , która tam zimować nie może .

{Powtóre} , iak mi oprócz {pobocznych} ‹Xiężna› sam graf Bezborodko twierdził , Cesarz nie na wiele zakrawa ; a ‹se› ‹dle› ‹r› {explikuiąc} ‹me› ‹ni› niby dla czego dotąd Herbert ‹nia› ‹y› nie dekuryerował , powiedział mi , że to ztąd {pochodzi} , że Pan iego chce wprzód bydz we {wszeiakiey} do attaku gotowości y o sposobie iego znieść się z Rossyą : co , przydał , wtenczas nastąpi , gdy woysko Rossyiskie wkroczy do Tartaryi Oczakowskiey y do Mołdawii ; że Cesarz będzie może attakować Turków y od Pokucia , zapewniwszy się , że nasz Kamieniec także {opatrzony} . Na te o Kamieńcu wspomnienie nie rzekłem , iż kiedy Rossyiskie woysko wkroczy do Mołdawii , {teatrum} woyny przeniesie się ku Dunaiowi . ‹Zgoda› ‹ła› tuteysze y Austryackie dyskursa {zdaią} się w {sobie} {zawierać} , że obydwa Cesarskie Dwory Turków razem przycisną , aby ich natychmiast do pokoiu ‹M.› ‹Miłł› musić .

Po trzecie . Nie tak łatwo przyidzie do złączenia się Francyi z Rossyą . Iuż tu mówią , iż w takowym razie niechybnie Anglia ‹złączyłaby› się z Królem Pruskim . M. ‹Saint-Saphorin› antifrancuzki przepowiadał mi wniyście Flotty Angielskiey do Baltyckie , która morze , y {zniszczenie} handlu Rossyiskiego ; ale z drugiey strony ten sam Minister Duński uformował w myśli ‹ści› ‹iey› drugi plan , na który ia bym prędzey przystał , iak na pokazanie się Flotty Angielskiey na morzu Baltyckim ; a ten plan iest takowy : że Rossya podług swoiey polityki będzie chciała menażować y Anglią y Francyą .

Ségur iest {iedynym} prawie , który z szacunkiem mówi o Xięciu de Nassau ; mnie samemu powiadał , żeby sobie był życzył , aby Xiąże de {Nassau} przyiechał do Petersburga wprzód , nim się udał do Xięcia Potemkina ; lecz że teraz iest tego zdania , aby się od wspomnionego Xięcia nie oddalał . Oprócz zaś Segura , tuteysi nawet Ministrowie , byle tylko wzmianka była o Nassau , naygrywaią się z niego .

À Capite . Przed ‹killka› ‹o› ‹1398› ‹mi› wystano ztąd przy konwoiu do FeldMarszałka Romanzoffa dwakroć {sto} tysięcy Rubli w Rossyiskiey ‹2› ‹cie› złotey y srebrney . Ta suma destynowana na płacenie u Nas prowiantów .

À Capite . Pogłoska o pożyczce 5 {milionów} w Banku Berlińskim była podobno umyślnie ‹roz› ‹to› ‹a› ‹na› przez Berlin dla ‹zboże› ‹a› ‹ła› ‹Minist› ‹ce› ‹nia› ; ale że iest nieprawdziwa , to pewna .

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

piece p37, Deboli, 16 Oct 1787 (the part in code 444 only) read in part

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (90% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# deboli1787 p37 - reading by sense (Deboli, 16 Oct 1787 (the part in code 444 only))

Marks: plain = certain; {x} = probable; [x] = restored; ‹x› = unresolved (decoded syllables that make no sense in place).

43 probable, 0 restored, 70 unresolved -> 0.903: read in part.

Dla maiącego dziś nastąpić wyiazdu mego do Gaczyny , {piszę} tę depeszę z rana . Do tey pory nie wiem ieszcze o przybyciu Kuryera w liście W. Kr. Mci de 3 currentis wzmiankowanego . Że go zaś ‹Xiądz› ‹w› ‹ye› ‹nie› ‹1216› , o tym zapewnić mogę . W niebytności podkanclerzego , który odiechał w Piątek na ‹yo› dla zabawienia tam przez kilka ‹1398› , zdarzyło mi się {rozmawiać} , choć dyskursem ‹I› ‹we› ‹ące› , dokładnie z Panem Markowem o kontentach wspomnioney depeszy . Wszystkiego , com na fundamencie oney {mówił} , słuchano z powolnością , y z oświadczeniem nawet , że dotąd trwa w ogulności chęć ocalenia Nas od wszelkiego {uszczerbku} z okazyi teraźnieyszey woyny y żądanie weyścia z ‹zwycięstwo› w umowy ; ale gdy przyszło do punktu warowania dla Nas części Kraiu w liście W. Kr. Mci {wymienionego} , tak mię zaraz okrzykniono , żem natychmiast postrzegł , że to tu z razu uderzy , gdy przyidzie wraz z planem tą z grafem ‹Hussar› ułożoną . ‹Stu› ‹ch› ‹ambicya› tedy {z} {natężeniem} dyssertacya , do którego to dało powód , a ta była {następuiąca} .

‹Wą› iestem WPan , rzeczono mi , dość długo w interessach , żebyś nie wiedział , że ‹napię› ta rzecz była przez Dwór Rossyiski podczas ostatniey woyny {tureckiey} , aby Polskę dzielić , lecz do tego okoliczności przymusiły Rossyą . W teraźnieyszym razie , gdy z strony waszey rzeczy {zabrużdżone} będą przez wzniecanie trudności , może podobnie wypaść , że choć poniewolnie zezwolić będziemy musieli na {uszczerbienie} wasze . Nie to ‹wa› ‹s› ‹ya› {zaprzątać} {powinno} , abyście Kray wasz rozszerzali , ale żebyście ten zachowali , który {posiadacie} . ‹Wiesz› , że WPan {spytano} mię się , co to za obszerność Kraiu , którego nabycia żądacie ? W tym mieyscu przerwałem , mówiąc , że znam mappę ; po którey to ogłoszone ‹honor› ‹y› ‹nu› ‹owa› ‹no› dyskurs w tey {osnowie} , iż nayprzód Rossya nie może przypuścić warunku o tym Kraiu , który ieszcze {niepodbity} ; że snadniey będzie wydzielić co Polsce z {zawoiowanych} nad Turkami , choć w prawdzie nie tyle ‹na› ‹y› ‹my› zakrawamy ; lecz że ta myśl {powinna} ‹na› ‹I› ‹ści› teraz na stronę , a bydz raczey natężona na dochowanie ‹akt› ‹al› ‹onych› {possessyi} Polskich ; że w ułożeniu pomnożenia onych to będzie nayszkodliwszym , iż gdy ten zamiar rozgłosi się , {sprawi} w umysłach natężenie , od którego ie ‹chętnie› ‹o› będzie odwrócić .

Gdy tedy dyssertacya do końca doprowadzona była , odpowiedziałem na ‹to› ‹ni› ‹aią› , dzieląc ią na {dwie} części . Na pierwszą część dotykaiącą {brużdżenia} u Nas , takem się ‹swo› ‹a› ‹em› ‹ł› , iż temu {brużdżeniu} {zapobieży} się przez przystąpienie Rossyi do srzodków , które ma ‹ktore› bydz użyte do utrzymania Polski pod hasłem iedności , że gdy na srzodkach {schodzić} będzie , {tudzież} na tym {przeświadczeniu} , które Rossya {wpoić} może , to iest : że od niey zawisło Polski konserwacya , może łatwo nastąpić nasz uszczerbek , którym Nas iednak {straszyć} nie należy , bo iuż się tak naród z tym {strachem} oswoił , że kiedy widzieć będzie , że Rossya nic dla niego nie robi , weźmie to samo za znak , że go opuszcza , a wtenczas partykularni tu y owdzie ‹rozstrychną› się , iak błędni , którzy szukaiąc ratunku w sidła wpadaią : ‹zuchwa› że ia tego zabłąkania właśnie widzę podobieństwo . Nazwano to desperacką myślą , lecz że iest niepodobna , ‹aią› tego nie utrzymywano .

Na drugą część {wspomnioney} dyssertacyi takem {odpowiedział} , że brać nie należy naszego zamiaru w {uzyskaniu} iako ‹we› ‹y› ‹domyśl› Kraiu nad Turkami w tym znaczeniu , iakoby to była z naszey strony kondycya {sine} {qua} {non} , albo trudność {wszczęta} dla odrażenia ; że o to bardzo proszę , aby to nie podpadło ‹da› ‹ło› opacznemu tłumaczeniu y podeyrzewaniu o iakową ‹swykwintności› ; że na odstąpienie od tego zamiaru y zapobieżenie , aby się nie rozgłos , krótki , dosyć będzie na {przestrodze} , którą dam w tey mierze , kiedy ieszcze wspomniona propozycya wiadoma ledwo kilku osobom , które wpływały w ułożenie planu , ‹ty› maiącey bydz przez Pana Stackelberga przysłaney , która zapewne nie będzie {rozgłaszana} , ponieważ ten {cały} układ miał bydz trzymany do {pewnego} czasu w sekrecie , zalecony mi tu przez podkanclerzego .

Iuż ‹ci› ‹hamulec› to wyraźnie widzę , miłościwy Panie , że mi w tey kategoryi nie inaczey wypadnie mówić z podkanclerzym , tylko w tey osnowie , iż ‹nio› ‹ze› ‹y› ‹Finlandya› ‹no› ‹szę› ‹ze› ‹m› mówić z Panem Markowem , dodaiąc to , że naylepiey będzie , gdy {nam} po odebraniu naszey planty , ‹ze› chcą szczerze powiedzieć , co im się zda lub nie . Co do upewnienia , te mi Jmć Pan Markow na końcu nawet rozmowy naszey powtórzył , o trwaiących dotąd dyspozycyach do ‹u› ‹dzieć› ‹nia› ‹ya› nami ułożenia , które w artykule użycia Woyska naszego przeciwko Turkom nazwał nasuniętym zaięciem umysłów potrzebnym do odwrócenia ich od szkodliwych związków .

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

piece g1, Gołuchowski, 13 Feb 1787 read in part

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (79% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# deboli1787 - English summaries of the pieces read

These are **summaries in English of the parts read by sense**, not full translations: every piece is read in part
(69-91% of words), and the unresolved stretches are left out. Names marked probable in the readings are probable here
too. Written by AI agents for Brice Dudout, 5-6 Nov 2026, from the readings in reading/; not checked by a historian.

## Deboli to the King

### p01 (29 Dec 1786)
Deboli tells the King that the Vice-Chancellor's (Ostermann's) evasive answer about the Russian regiments stationed in Poland kept him from asking formally, for fear of an explicit refusal; he considers an approach through Potemkin and mentions abuses committed by the regiments. Last Wednesday he pressed the Vice-Chancellor, who said neither yes nor no, so the silence has to be read. Since the King's despatch of the 16th, he sees that Russia is not disposed to withdraw the regiments. At the next conference he will use the Department's despatch of the 9th and the argument that the Emperor, travelling to Kiev, will see Russian regiments in Poland with his own eyes. Partly read passages: Buchholtz (the Prussian envoy) spreading a "falsehood"; a visitor explaining his interests; a count's memorial on estates of Polish families in Ukraine. To the Department he writes only pro forma.

### p04 (9 Jan 1787)
Deboli expects an answer at tomorrow's conference on the King's letter of the 16th (Dec.). The aim is finally to be rid of the regiment in question (cf. piece 1). The Empress's departure remains as announced. By the last Vienna courier, Joseph II will come to Kherson rather than Kiev and cannot stay long because the Neapolitan sovereigns are expected in Vienna. Potemkin will go to Krichev (his estate) to receive the Empress and will wait for her at Kiev; otherwise he could not finish his tours. The affair of the Princess of Württemberg (Augusta of Brunswick) troubles the grand-ducal couple: there was talk of sending her to Reval during the Empress's absence pending an answer from her father the Duke of Brunswick, who reportedly wrote urging her to win her husband's affection and declines to take her side or recall her; since yesterday it is said the Empress will take her along. A general, taken seriously ill on Saturday, is better. Deboli received the King's letter of 7 December yesterday.

### p07 (18 Jan 1787)
Deboli reports a talk with Vice-Chancellor Ostermann about the Russian regiment stationed in Poland. Deboli presses for its withdrawal, as the Sejm instructed him. Ostermann answers that the Poles complain now of the Emperor, now of the King of Prussia, while also demanding the withdrawal. He adds that the regiment keeps the peace and that the Hetman himself complained of it. He blames Deboli's vehemence and tells him to put these points to the King. Deboli notes that he has waited for an answer for almost a year. For three years, he says, he has answered foreign envoys vaguely. The French minister, speaking to the Dutch one, assumed the King would raise the matter when he met the Empress.

In Deboli's view everything depends on Potemkin, who wants the affair sent to a Commission. Deboli fears such a Commission: the grievances (Igelström, a detachment entering a district in 1783) would be turned back against Poland. He suggests the King write to Potemkin before Kiev. He will not use the King's letter of the 3rd. He also mentions Potemkin's plan to buy an estate in Poland ("S mila", probably Smiła).

The letter then covers:
- the affair of the Palatine of Ruthenia, with Chernyshev, General Komarzewski and Razumovsky;
- a young castellan's son who came to St Petersburg via Riga with neither his father's leave nor a passport. He claimed to carry a letter from the Hetman to Potemkin. Deboli refuses to protect him, lends him 300 roubles against a bond and sends him home via Połock.

### p13, p15, p21
- **13 (27/7)** — Foreign ministers await an audience with the Empress. She received three of them at Tsarskoye Selo, in a small circle and in very good humour. Hetman Branicki and his wife are to come to St Petersburg this winter (news from Cobenzl). There are hopes he will regain the King's favour. Deboli answers that the King does not push him away.
- **15 (31/8)** — Three posts came with no letter. The answer to the King's letter will come from the Empress herself, since Ostermann's chancery has no orders. The British envoy (Fitzherbert) has left and was given the Empress's portrait. On Tuesday Ségur's tragedy "Coriolan" was staged at the Hermitage, with only Cobenzl and the author invited. Comedies and proverbs are played in the small theatre; the other ministers come on Thursdays. Ségur leaves within a week with the Prince de Ligne, after M. de Sainte-Croix's arrival. The letter is left unsigned as Deboli leaves for Pavlovsk.
- **21 (17/8)** — Deboli asks how the King's letter and its allegorical idea were received. The Empress came back to town, without saying whether she would return to Tsarskoye Selo. A ceremony was held at the Summer Palace for the Preobrazhensky regiment's feast. She then moved into the Winter Palace, to everyone's satisfaction.

### p23, p28, p30
- **23 (14/9)** — Courier to Warsaw; Cobenzl and the Austrian courier; the Turks have declared war; French mediation; Ostermann on France and Berlin; Sardinia; Prussian recruitment (2 × 500); the prince de Ligne wishes to write to the king.
- **28 (25/9)** — Back from Gatchina; the king's letter of the 12th; Cobenzl says the alliance binds the Emperor to war; Council debate on peace with the Turks; Ligne's letter sent with the Austrian courier; Ligne asks about an extraordinary sejm; Deboli jokes he will propose him as deputy for Wilno.
- **30 (3/8)** — Pisarz Wielhorski has arrived on business of the castellan's wife Ogińska of Wilno (probable), with the chancellor's letters to the vice-chancellor, Count Bezborodko and Prince Vyazemsky (probable); Deboli has introduced him. Starosta Potocki: 200,000 roubles would have been lost had they not been withdrawn, merchants having failed (partly unresolved); creditors will get barely 5 %. Potocki still loses through the exchange rate, down to 36 stuivers (probable) per rouble against 42 at the start of winter, on the 400,000 roubles he sent.

### p33, p37
- **33 (7 Oct.)** — At tomorrow's conference Deboli will carry out the king's order (dispatch of the 24th of last month): Field Marshal Rumyantsev's division has hastened its entry into Turkish territory to screen Poland; he will ask when to expect the answer on the alliance project with Russia ("in hand"); there is hesitation here about starting anything in Poland and fear of trouble from Prussia; Deboli answers that inaction would be taken as abandoning Poland and would help Prussian intrigues. Three conclusions: 1) no long war is wanted: take Ochakov (this autumn or rather in winter), then make peace; 2) according to Bezborodko the Emperor will do little; Herbert has not yet sent a courier because Joseph II wants to be ready and to concert with Russia; the attack will come when the Russian army enters Ochakov Tartary and Moldavia, the Emperor also attacking from Pokutia if Kamieniec is secured; the theatre of war will move to the Danube; 3) a Franco-Russian union is hard: England would join Prussia; the Danish minister (Saint-Saphorin, probable) foretells an English fleet in the Baltic, but has a second plan: Russia will humour both England and France. Ségur is almost the only one to speak of the prince of Nassau with respect; ministers here mock him. 200,000 roubles in gold and silver sent under escort to Rumyantsev to pay for provisions in Poland. Rumour of a 5-million loan (probable) at the Berlin bank, spread on purpose by Berlin, and false.
- **37 (16 Oct.), 444 part** — Written in the morning before leaving for Gatchina; the courier announced in the king's letter of the 3rd has not arrived. With the vice-chancellor away, a long talk with Markov on the dispatch: he is heard kindly and told Russia still wants to shield Poland from any damage in this war and to come to an agreement; but on guaranteeing a territory for Poland (asked in the king's letter) Markov protests and gives a "dissertation": in the last Turkish war circumstances forced Russia into the partition; if Poland makes difficulties Russia might again have to consent to its loss; the aim should be keeping, not enlarging, the country; Russia cannot promise unconquered land; anything would rather come from conquests over the Turks; above all Polish possessions must be preserved; spreading the plan would be most harmful. Deboli answers in two parts: 1) troubles in Poland will be prevented if Russia joins in the means of keeping Poland under the watchword of union; the nation, used to fear, would read Russian inaction as abandonment, and individuals would scatter "like lost men who fall into snares while seeking help"; 2) the idea of gains from the Turks is no sine qua non; he asks that it not be misconstrued; the proposal, known to few, is to come through Stackelberg and stay secret, as the vice-chancellor urged. He must now treat the matter with the vice-chancellor on these lines; Markov repeats that the disposition to an agreement remains and calls the use of the Polish army against the Turks a way to occupy minds and turn them from harmful ties. The rest of the piece is in other tables (9933, 4000): not read.

## Gołuchowski to a minister of the King

### g1 (13 Feb 1787)
Gołuchowski has nothing to add on the Empress's journey beyond his report to the Department. He denies what was reported about Prince Repnin (a sum of roubles and a place in the suite): it is untrue. Buchholtz reportedly announced the coming entry of 10 Russian regiments, causing alarm in Warsaw; questioned by the Prussian minister in St Petersburg, Gołuchowski said what he knew and refrained from saying more, since the matter was not clear enough in the report of the Palatine of Ruthenia.

### g3 (30 Mar 1787)
On Sunday at court the foreign ministers (Danish, St-Saphorin; Portuguese, Horta?) pressed him about the 10 regiments said to be entering Poland; he answered he had nothing certain. The Danish minister then asked about Şahin Giray: Hamburg papers said he would be sent to Persia and later return to the Crimea, where the Empress is to go. Gołuchowski said he knew no more than the Hamburg papers and ended the conversation to avoid further questions.

### g4 (18 May 1787)
Charged (by Prince Stanisław Poniatowski's household) with finding out Grand Duke Paul's taste in saddle horses to be sent from Poland, Gołuchowski questioned the riding master who had been at Tsarskoye Selo. The horse must be: 1) very calm; 2) unafraid of firing, attacks, arms drill, soldiers' shouts and cannon — not even moving its head when the Grand Duke, as he does, rides up to the guns; 3) lively when needed and well schooled; 4) sound and strong-legged, since the Grand Duke rides his chosen horse every day: he sets off at a gallop to a fixed point, then walks, out twice a day, sometimes up to 40 versts. The Grand Duke complained he had no good horse to sit before his regiment's front without fear and wants a Polish horse for the May review (Old Style). Gołuchowski objects that horses sent on 15 May will only arrive on 26 June; and he doubts that one horse can have all these qualities (the rest of the reading is in reading/g4.md, in Polish).

piece g3, Gołuchowski, 30 Mar 1787 read in part

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (70% here); the holes are marked in the reading.
